Reverse all positions in a fixed-width 32-bit word and return its unsigned integer value.

## Problem

**Input.** An unsigned integer `value` from 0 through 4294967295, interpreted as exactly 32 bits.

**Output.** Return the unsigned integer represented by those 32 bits in reverse order.

**Rules.** Reverse bit positions, not decimal digits or byte order.
Leading zero bits are part of the word and become trailing zeros after reversal.
The output remains a 32-bit unsigned value. Negative inputs require a different declared-width interpretation below.

**Example.** Input `1` has only its least significant bit set; return `2147483648`, with only the highest bit set.
Input `0` returns `0`.

## Picture

Start smaller: reverse the eight-bit pattern for six.

| Bit position | 7 | 6 | 5 | 4 | 3 | 2 | 1 | 0 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Input | 0 | 0 | 0 | 0 | 0 | 1 | 1 | 0 |
| Reversed | 0 | 1 | 1 | 0 | 0 | 0 | 0 | 0 |

Read low bits `0,1,1`; the answer progresses `0,1,3`. The remaining input is now zero, but five positions remain.

Appending those five zeros shifts three into `01100000`, which is 96. Stopping at numeric zero would incorrectly return three.

## Code

The original implementation applies the same transition exactly 32 times:

```python
def reverse_bits(value: int) -> int:
   answer = 0
   for _ in range(32):
      answer = (answer << 1) | (value & 1)
      value >>= 1
   return answer
```

`answer << 1` opens one low destination position. OR fills it with the current low input bit. Then `value >>= 1` removes that input position.

After `k` iterations, the answer reverses the original low-`k` block. The remaining input equals the original shifted right by `k`.

The counter retains the block's width even when its numeric value is zero.

For the fixed 32-bit contract, time and space are $O(1)$. The input integer is not mutated in the caller.

**Recall:** What determines termination? The declared width, not the remaining numeric value or its bit length.

<!-- learning:core-end -->

class="coding-visual-lesson"><dt>Recognize it</dt><dd data-coding-review="recognitionCue">The task reverses all 32 positions, including leading zeros, so processing must run a fixed number of times rather than stopping when the numeric input becomes zero.</dd></div><div class="coding-visual-lesson"><dt>Keep true</dt><dd data-coding-review="invariant">After k iterations, answer contains the original k least-significant bits in reverse read order, while value contains the original bits not yet consumed after k right shifts.</dd></div><div class="coding-visual-lesson"><dt>Reuse it</dt><dd data-coding-review="transferLesson">For fixed-width encodings, separate reading order from numeric termination: consume one boundary unit, shift the destination, append, and repeat for the declared width. The pattern transfers to radix conversion and stream packing.</dd></div></dl></div><figcaption><strong>Read it this way:</strong> Input 43261596 is 00000010100101000001111010011100₂. Set answer = 0 before reading bit position 0. Step through the frames to watch the state change. The last frame shows the answer or the stopping condition. <span class="visual-reader-check" data-reader-check="reverse-bits-state"><strong>Check:</strong> Why must the loop continue after iteration 26 when the remaining input is already zero?</span></figcaption></figure>

## Declare Another Width

**Choose this when:** Width is an interface parameter rather than the fixed value 32.

Mask to the low `width` bits first. This defines negative inputs modulo $2^{\text{width}}$ instead of treating them as unbounded signed bit strings.

```python
def reverse_bits_width(value, width):
   if width < 0:
      raise ValueError("negative width")
   value &= (1 << width) - 1
   answer = 0
   for _ in range(width):
      answer = (answer << 1) | (value & 1)
      value >>= 1
   return answer
```

Integer width zero returns zero. A negative width raises `ValueError`. At width eight, six becomes 96 and `-1` becomes 255.

Two reversals recover the original low-width pattern. Discarded high bits are not recovered.

The helper performs exactly `width` iterations. Python's shifts and integer storage depend on width; they are not fixed-bit constant costs.

## Reverse Bytes, Not Individual Bits

**Choose this when:** The encoding changes byte significance order while preserving bits inside each byte.

Consume eight bits at a time and append an eight-bit unit. Require a nonnegative integer `byte_count`.

```python
def reverse_byte_order(value, byte_count):
   answer = 0
   for _ in range(byte_count):
      answer = (answer << 8) | (value & 255)
      value >>= 8
   return answer
```

Four-byte `0x12345678` becomes `0x78563412`. The same width applied to one gives `0x01000000`.

Extra high bits are ignored. Zero bytes give zero. This helper performs one iteration per byte, with width-dependent Python arithmetic and storage.

## Transfer

Bit reversal maps position `j` to `width - 1 - j`. Byte reversal applies that permutation to eight-bit units instead.

Choosing the unit and width is part of the task, not a cosmetic representation choice.
